It just... it just seems to be there in the background most of the time, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really do. My mind just keeps going from one concern to another. I'll be worrying about my sister in Arecibo, and then I'm thinking about whether I missed something at the office, or if my husband is safe driving home... or my church commitments, or the bills. I just find myself constantly preparing for problems that haven’t even happened yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had anything like that. It doesn't... it doesn't just hit me all at once like a sudden wave or anything. Like I said, it's more of just a constant, low-level hum of worry in the background. But no sudden, intense fear like you're describing, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries or tension?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I still do everything I normally would... going to work, doing my church commitments, running errands. The worry is just kind of there while I'm doing those things. But I wouldn't say I actually avoid any situations or activities because of it. I just push through it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually about one particular thing or are they spread across many different topics?\n\nPatient: Like I mentioned earlier, it's really spread out across a lot of different things. It's not just one specific thing that sets me off. It's my sister, my job, my husband, the bills... it's just a bunch of different topics all jumbled together.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is greater than would be expected for the situations you're worrying about?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is. I mean, my mind just keeps going from one concern to another. I'll be worrying about my sister down in Arecibo, and then suddenly I'm stressing over whether I missed something at the office, or if my husband is safe driving home. Then it's on to my church commitments, or the bills... it just doesn't stop. And honestly, I find myself preparing for problems that haven't even happened yet. So, yes, it probably is more than what most people would expect.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So it is affecting things, even if I'm still managing to get through the day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been experiencing this constant worry?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / computer_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about thirteen months, since my sister in Arecibo started needing much more help from the family.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I came in because I just feel wound up almost all day. It's like... my body is constantly bracing for something. And it happens even when I’m just sitting at my computer working, or trying to relax after dinner. I just can't seem to shake that tense feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical pain when you feel this tension?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't really call it pain. It's more just... well, tightness, I guess. It doesn't actually hurt anywhere, I just feel very stiff.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of tension seem to happen at specific times or in specific situations?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven't noticed any real pattern to it. Like I mentioned, it can happen when I'm just sitting at my desk working, or even when I'm supposed to be relaxing in the evening. It just... it just seems to be there in the background most of the time, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really do. My mind just keeps going from one concern to another. I'll be worrying about my sister in Arecibo, and then I'm thinking about whether I missed something at the office, or if my husband is safe driving home... or my church commitments, or the bills. I just find myself constantly preparing for problems that haven’t even happened yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had anything like that. It doesn't... it doesn't just hit me all at once like a sudden wave or anything. Like I said, it's more of just a constant, low-level hum of worry in the background. But no sudden, intense fear like you're describing, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries or tension?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I still do everything I normally would... going to work, doing my church commitments, running errands. The worry is just kind of there while I'm doing those things. But I wouldn't say I actually avoid any situations or activities because of it. I just push through it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually about one particular thing or are they spread across many different topics?\n\nPatient: Like I mentioned earlier, it's really spread out across a lot of different things. It's not just one specific thing that sets me off. It's my sister, my job, my husband, the bills... it's just a bunch of different topics all jumbled together.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is greater than would be expected for the situations you're worrying about?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is. I mean, my mind just keeps going from one concern to another. I'll be worrying about my sister down in Arecibo, and then suddenly I'm stressing over whether I missed something at the office, or if my husband is safe driving home. Then it's on to my church commitments, or the bills... it just doesn't stop. And honestly, I find myself preparing for problems that haven't even happened yet. So, yes, it probably is more than what most people would expect.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm still getting my work done, and I'm showing up for my family and church commitments. But it does interfere a bit, I suppose. I've noticed it takes me longer to finish my reports at work now, just because... well, I double-check things way more than I used to. And with my relationships, the tension kind of spills over sometimes. I've actually had to apologize a few times lately for sounding short with the people I care about. So it is affecting things, even if I'm still managing to get through the day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for... I'd say about thirteen months now. It really started back when my sister down in Arecibo began needing a lot more help from our family.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel your worry doesn't interfere at all?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it kind of touches everything in some way. Even when I'm just trying to relax or do something simple, the worry is always humming there in the background. So I can't think of any part of my life where it doesn't at least show up a little bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless when you're worried?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Actually, that's a big part of why I decided to come in. I just feel wound up almost all day long. It's like my body is constantly bracing for something to happen, even when I'm just sitting at my computer for work, or trying to relax in the evening after dinner. I just can't seem to shake that tense feeling.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worries are focused on just a few main topics or are they more scattered across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: They are definitely scattered. My mind just keeps going from one concern to another, really. Then it's my church commitments, or the bills... it just jumps around all over the place. I basically find myself constantly preparing for problems that haven't even happened yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are realistic reasons for the level of worry you have about these various topics?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... logically, I know there isn't. My husband is a safe driver, and things at the office are generally fine. So there's no real reason for me to be this worried about all of it. I know the amount I worry is out of proportion to what's actually going on, but I just can't seem to convince my brain of that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So, yes, it probably is more than what most people would expect.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry been interfering with your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm still getting my work done, and I'm showing up for my family and church commitments. But it does interfere a bit, I suppose. I've noticed it takes me longer to finish my reports at work now, just because... well, I double-check things way more than I used to. And with my relationships, the tension kind of spills over sometimes. I've actually had to apologize a few times lately for sounding short with the people I care about. So it is affecting things, even if I'm still managing to get through the day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worry?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for... I'd say about thirteen months now. It really started back when my sister down in Arecibo began needing a lot more help from our family.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel your worry doesn't interfere at all?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it kind of touches everything in some way. Even when I'm just trying to relax or do something simple, the worry is always humming there in the background. So I can't think of any part of my life where it doesn't at least show up a little bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless when you're worried?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Actually, that's a big part of why I decided to come in. I just feel wound up almost all day long. It's like my body is constantly bracing for something to happen, even when I'm just sitting at my computer for work, or trying to relax in the evening after dinner. I just can't seem to shake that tense feeling.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worries are focused on just a few main topics or are they more scattered across many different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: They are definitely scattered. My mind just keeps going from one concern to another, really. Um, it'll be about my sister in Arecibo, and then I'll start worrying about whether I missed something on my computer at the office, or if my husband is safe driving home. Then it's my church commitments, or the bills... it just jumps around all over the place. I basically find myself constantly preparing for problems that haven't even happened yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are realistic reasons for the level of worry you have about these various topics?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... logically, I know there isn't. My husband is a safe driver, and things at the office are generally fine. So there's no real reason for me to be this worried about all of it. I know the amount I worry is out of proportion to what's actually going on, but I just can't seem to convince my brain of that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worry is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it a lot of distress, no. Um, it's not like it's completely ruining my life or keeping me from doing my job. It's more just... really frustrating. I get tired of always feeling so on edge and having my mind race, but I wouldn't say I'm severely distressed by it. It's just bothersome enough that I wanted to finally talk to someone and see if I could get it to stop.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Not significantly, no. I'm still getting my work done and showing up for my family and my church. But... I guess it does have some effect. It takes me longer to finish my reports at the office because I end up double-checking things a lot more than I used to. And, well, I have had to apologize a few times lately for sounding a bit short with people I care about. So it doesn't stop me from doing what I need to do, it just makes it all a bit harder.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just can't seem to shake that tense feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical pain when you feel this tension?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't really call it pain. It's more just... well, tightness, I guess. It doesn't actually hurt anywhere, I just feel very stiff.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of tension seem to happen at specific times or in specific situations?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I haven't noticed any real pattern to it. Like I mentioned, it can happen when I'm just sitting at my desk working, or even when I'm supposed to be relaxing in the evening. My mind just keeps going from one concern to another. I'll be worrying about my sister in Arecibo, and then I'm thinking about whether I missed something at the office, or if my husband is safe driving home... or my church commitments, or the bills. I just find myself constantly preparing for problems that haven’t even happened yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had anything like that. It doesn't... it doesn't just hit me all at once like a sudden wave or anything. Like I said, it's more of just a constant, low-level hum of worry in the background. But no sudden, intense fear like you're describing, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worries or tension?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I still do everything I normally would... going to work, doing my church commitments, running errands. The worry is just kind of there while I'm doing those things. But I wouldn't say I actually avoid any situations or activities because of it. I just push through it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually about one particular thing or are they spread across many different topics?\n\nPatient: Like I mentioned earlier, it's really spread out across a lot of different things. It's not just one specific thing that sets me off. It's my sister, my job, my husband, the bills... it's just a bunch of different topics all jumbled together.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worry is greater than would be expected for the situations you're worrying about?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is. I mean, my mind just keeps going from one concern to another. I'll be worrying about my sister down in Arecibo, and then suddenly I'm stressing over whether I missed something at the office, or if my husband is safe driving home. Then it's on to my church commitments, or the bills... it just doesn't stop. And honestly, I find myself preparing for problems that haven't even happened yet. I'd say about thirteen months now. Then it's my church commitments, or the bills... it just jumps around all over the place. I mean... logically, I know there isn't. My husband is a safe driver, and things at the office are generally fine. So there's no real reason for me to be this worried about all of it. I know the amount I worry is out of proportion to what's actually going on, but I just can't seem to convince my brain of that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your worry is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it a lot of distress, no.
